Now I have the full picture. Here's my analysis: --- `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` The architecture already has per-slot-per-lock dirty tracking — each `SlotSyncManager` instance is scoped to a single `(slot, lock)` pair with its own `_dirty: bool`. The dirty-on-every-tick-check-and-retry loop you're describing is essentially what already exists. The real problem is **two layers deeper**. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` ## Root Cause Analysis There are **two bugs** working together to produce the issue in #1061: ### Bug 1: Error misclassification in the Z-Wave provider When `get_usercode_from_node()` fails at `zwave_js.py:448` (the V1 post-set poll), it throws a raw `FailedZWaveCommand` from the zwave_js_server library. This is **not** a subclass of `LockDisconnected` or `LockOperationFailed`, so it falls through to the generic `except Exception` handler at `sync.py:581`, which treats it as an unexpected bug and immediately disables the slot. Z-Wave errors 202 ("node presumed dead") and 204 ("node did not acknowledge") are textbook transient connectivity failures — they should be caught and wrapped as `LockDisconnected` in the provider. ### Bug 2: `_disable_slot` has profile-wide blast radius When any single lock's `SlotSyncManager` calls `_disable_slot()`, it turns off the slot's **profile-level enabled switch** (`async_disable_slot` in `util.py:34`). Since the enabled switch is shared across all locks in the profile, one lock's failure disables the slot for every lock — even locks that successfully synced. ## Why per-slot dirty markers alone don't fix it You already have per-slot-per-lock dirty markers. The dirty/retry loop works correctly for the `LockDisconnected` path (`sync.py:572-580`) — it sets `self._dirty = True` and retries on the next tick. The problem is that the errors never reach that handler. ## Recommended Fix (two parts) ### Part 1: Catch `FailedZWaveCommand` in the Z-Wave provider In `async_set_usercode` and `async_clear_usercode`, wrap the `get_usercode_from_node` call (and potentially the service call itself) to catch `FailedZWaveCommand` and re-raise as `LockDisconnected`. This routes transient Z-Wave failures …

> AGENT

Good — so `BaseLockCodeManagerCodeSlotPerLockEntity._is_available()` already checks if that single lock's entity is unavailable. And the `InSyncBinarySensorEntity.available` at line 269-273 inherits this. So the in-sync sensor **already goes unavailable when the lock entity is unavailable in HA**. The problem is a **timing gap**: the Z-Wave command fails with `FailedZWaveCommand` *before* HA marks the lock entity as unavailable, and the sync manager immediately disables the slot profile-wide. Here's what "unavailable" should look like in this scenario: 1. **Error classification fix**: `FailedZWaveCommand` with node-dead/unresponsive errors → `LockDisconnected` → existing retry path (`dirty = True`, try again next tick) 2. **Circuit breaker fix**: When the circuit breaker trips (3 failures in 5 minutes), instead of calling `_disable_slot` (profile-wide), the `SlotSyncManager` should enter a **suspended state** — stop retrying and let the in-sync sensor report unavailable for *just that lock*. When the lock reconnects (coordinator refresh fires, which calls `_mark_dirty`), it resumes. 3. **Unexpected errors**: Similarly, the generic `except Exception` handler shouldn't disable the slot profile-wide. It could either: - Treat truly unexpected errors the same as the circuit breaker (suspend that lock/slot), or - Create a per-lock repair issue without touching the enabled switch The key insight is: **`_disable_slot` should almost never be called from a single lock's sync manager**, because it's a profile-wide action. The only legitimate caller would be something like `CodeRejectedError` where the *code itself* is bad (not the lock), and even that could be debated. Does this match your thinking? The fix is essentially: - Route Z-Wave transient errors into `LockDisconnected` - Replace `_disable_slot` calls in the circuit breaker and generic error handler with a per-lock suspend/unavailable mechanism - Let the existing reconnection flow (coordinator listener → `_mark_dirty`) handle auto-recovery

So disabling the slot makes sense there. I think the consistent framework is about **who/what is at fault**: | Category | Fault | Scope | Action | Auto-recovers? | |---|---|---|---|---| | **Code errors** | The PIN itself | Profile-wide | Disable slot | No — user must fix | | **Lock errors** | The lock/network | Per-lock | Unavailable + retry | Yes — on reconnect | **Code errors** (profile-wide disable is correct): - `CodeRejectedError` — firmware won't accept this PIN - `DuplicateCodeError` — PIN conflicts with another slot on this lock **Lock errors** (per-lock unavailable, retry): - `LockDisconnected` — can't reach lock - `LockOperationFailed` — lock reachable but operation failed - `FailedZWaveCommand` (currently uncaught) — Z-Wave transport failure - Circuit breaker trips — repeated transient failures - Generic `Exception` — unknown, but shouldn't nuke the whole profile for something we don't understand The consistency rule becomes: **only errors where the code/PIN is provably wrong should disable the slot. Everything else is a lock-level problem.** That means the current generic `Exception` handler and the circuit breaker are both wrong — they call `_disable_slot` for what are fundamentally lock-level problems. And `FailedZWaveCommand` just needs proper classification. The one nuance: the circuit breaker exists to prevent infinite retry loops. With the "unavailable" approach, the circuit breaker would stop retries and mark the lock/slot unavailable, and the coordinator reconnect listener would be the reset trigger — which is cleaner than a time-based window. Now I have enough context to propose approaches. Here are two: ## Approach A: Suspend state in SlotSyncManager (recommended) Add a `_suspended: bool` flag to `SlotSyncManager`. When the circuit breaker trips or a generic exception fires: - Set `_suspended = True` - Create a per-lock repair issue - Stop retrying (tick returns early when suspended) - Expose `sync_status` attribute on the in-sync binary sensor **Resume trigger**: `_mark_dirty` clears `_suspended` — this fires on coordinator updates (lock reconnects) and entity state changes (user changes PIN). This means any state change is a signal to try again. **Error classification fix**: Z-Wave provider wraps `FailedZWaveCommand` as `LockDisconnected`, routed into the existing retry path. **Pros**: Minimal structural change — adds one flag and one attribute, modifies error handlers in place. **Cons**: `_mark_dirty` doing double duty (dirty + unsuspend) is implicit. ## Approach B: State machine enum Replace the `_dirty` bool + `_suspended` bool + `_in_sync` bool with a single state enum (`LOADING`, `SYNCED`, `OUT_OF_SYNC`, `SYNCING`, `SUSPENDED`). Transitions are explicit and the `sync_status` attribute maps directly from the enum. **Pros**: Cleaner model, impossible to have contradictory flag combinations. **Cons**: Bigger refactor — touches every place that reads/writes `_dirty`, `_in_sync`. More risk, more churn for what's fundamentally a bug fix + one new feature. --- **My recommendation**: Approach A. The current flag model works, we're adding one flag and one attribute. Let me present the design section by section. ## Section 1: State Machine The enum replaces `_dirty`, `_in_sync`, and `_tick_in_sync` with a single source of truth: ``` LOADING → SYNCED LOADING → OUT_OF_SYNC → SYNCING → SYNCED ↑ ↓ ←─────────← (sync failed, retry) ↓ SUSPENDED (circuit breaker or unexpected error) ↓ OUT_OF_SYNC (state change resumes) ``` | State | Meaning | Tick behavior | `is_on` | `sync_status` | |---|---|---|---|---| | `LOADING` | Initial — waiting for valid entity states | Resolve state, transition | `None` | — | | `SYNCED` | Lock code matches desired | Skip (no work) | `True` | `synced` | | `OUT_OF_SYNC` | Mismatch detected, will attempt sync next tick | Transition to `SYNCING`, perform sync | `False` | `out_of_sync` | | `SYNCING` | Sync operation in progress this tick | N/A (transient, only during tick execution) | `False` | `out_of_sync` | | `SUSPENDED` | Circuit breaker tripped or unexpected error, retries paused | Skip (no work) | `False` | `suspended` | **Key behaviors:** - `_mark_dirty` becomes `_request_sync_check` — if state is `SYNCED`, transitions to `OUT_OF_SYNC`. If `SUSPENDED`, transitions to `OUT_OF_SYNC` (resume). If already `OUT_OF_SYNC` or `SYNCING`, no-op. - The tick only acts on `OUT_OF_SYNC` — transitions to `SYNCING`, runs the operation, then transitions to `SYNCED` or back to `OUT_OF_SYNC` (retry) or `SUSPENDED` (gave up). - Circuit breaker reset happens on state transitions (same as today's `_tick_in_sync` logic), but now it's an explicit `OUT_OF_SYNC → SYNCED` transition that resets the counter. Does this state model look right before I move to the next section?

> AGENT

I like the instinct, but I think OUT_OF_SYNC earns its place because of the tick architecture. Consider what happens when a state change fires while SYNCED: - The callback runs *between* ticks - The actual sync operation happens *during* the next tick (up to 5 seconds later) If we go SYNCED → SYNCING in the callback, we're claiming "sync in progress" when nothing is actually happening yet. OUT_OF_SYNC is the honest state: "we know there's a mismatch, we'll act on it next tick." It also matters for the dashboard — the user sees `out_of_sync` for those few seconds, which is accurate. Showing `syncing` when we're just waiting would be misleading. And internally, SYNCING prevents a second tick from starting a concurrent sync operation. If SYNCING also means "waiting for tick," that guard breaks down. So I'd keep them separate: - **OUT_OF_SYNC**: "mismatch detected, queued for next tick" (visible to user) - **SYNCING**: "sync operation executing right now" (transient, within a single tick) They both map to `sync_status: "out_of_sync"` on the attribute since the user doesn't need to distinguish them — it's an internal distinction that keeps the tick logic clean. Does that rationale hold up?

## Section 3: Resume and Circuit Breaker Behavior **Resuming from SUSPENDED**: Any call to `_request_sync_check` (the renamed `_mark_dirty`) transitions SUSPENDED → OUT_OF_SYNC. This fires on: - Coordinator updates (lock reconnects, push data) - Entity state changes (user changes PIN, toggles active switch) This means the user can also "retry" a suspended lock by simply toggling the slot or changing the PIN — no need for a separate "retry" mechanism. **Circuit breaker**: Same parameters as today (3 attempts in 5 minutes), but scoped differently: - Only counts attempts in the SYNCING state (set operations) - Tripping transitions SYNCING → SUSPENDED with a per-lock repair issue - The counter resets on any transition into SYNCED (sync succeeded) or when the circuit breaker window expires - `_disable_slot` is **never** called from the circuit breaker path **Per-lock repair issue**: The issue key changes from `slot_disabled_{entry_id}_{slot_num}` to `slot_suspended_{entry_id}_{slot_num}_{lock_entity_id}` — scoped to the specific lock so multiple locks can have independent issues. **Clearing the repair issue**: When a suspended lock resumes and successfully syncs (reaches SYNCED), the per-lock repair issue is automatically deleted. Does this section look right?

> AGENT

There's only one repair issue type — `slot_disabled` — used for everything. No separate duplicate code issue. So yes, we still need `slot_disabled` for the `CodeRejectedError`/`DuplicateCodeError` path since that's where `_disable_slot` correctly disables the profile-wide switch. Summary of repair issues in the new design: | Trigger | Issue key | Scope | |---|---|---| | `CodeRejectedError` / `DuplicateCodeError` | `slot_disabled_{entry_id}_{slot_num}` | Profile-wide (slot disabled) | | Circuit breaker / generic exception | `slot_suspended_{entry_id}_{slot_num}_{lock_entity_id}` | Per-lock (retries paused) | Both are fixable repair issues. The `slot_disabled` one clears when the user re-enables the slot. The `slot_suspended` one clears when the lock resumes and reaches SYNCED. ## Section 4: Summary of changes by file 1. **`sync.py`**: State machine enum replacing `_dirty`/`_in_sync`/`_tick_in_sync`. It's actually per lock *per slot* — a single lock could have slot 2 suspended (circuit breaker tripped after 3 failed attempts) while slot 3 syncs fine. Each `SlotSyncManager` is a `(slot, lock)` pair with its own circuit breaker. So I think the key should be `slot_suspended_{entry_id}_{slot_num}_{lock_entity_id}` to capture that granularity. Otherwise one slot's suspension would clobber another slot's repair issue on the same lock. Or are you thinking suspensions should be per-lock (all slots on that lock suspend together)?

yes per lock, because if it's a transient issue it will affect all slots. We should show alls lots impacted

Good point — if the lock is having communication issues, it'll fail across all slots. Per-lock suspension is cleaner. That changes the design: when a circuit breaker trips or a generic exception fires on any slot, we suspend **all slots on that lock** and create one repair issue per lock. When the lock recovers (coordinator update), all slots resume together. This means the suspension state needs to be coordinated across `SlotSyncManager` instances for the same lock. Right now each manager is independent. A few ways to handle this: 1. **Lock-level suspend flag on the coordinator or lock object** — all sync managers check it before acting. One manager trips it, all see it. 2. **Broadcast from the tripping manager** — when one slot suspends, it notifies sibling managers for the same lock to also transition to SUSPENDED. Option 1 feels cleaner — the suspend state lives where the scope is (the lock), not replicated across every slot manager. The `SlotSyncManager` tick checks the lock-level flag before transitioning from OUT_OF_SYNC → SYNCING. Which direction do you prefer?

> AGENT

Makes sense. So the updated design: **Suspend flag**: A `_suspended: bool` on `BaseLock` (or the coordinator). When any `SlotSyncManager` hits a circuit breaker or generic exception, it sets the lock-level flag. All sync managers for that lock check this flag in their tick — if suspended, they transition to SUSPENDED instead of attempting sync. **Resume**: The coordinator listener clears the lock-level flag when new data arrives (lock reconnects, state change). All sync managers see the flag cleared and transition SUSPENDED → OUT_OF_SYNC on their next tick. **Repair issue key**: `slot_suspended_{entry_id}_{lock_entity_id}` — one per lock, listing all affected slots in the description. This also simplifies the per-slot circuit breaker — instead of each slot independently counting to 3 and suspending, the first slot to trip suspends the whole lock. The other slots don't waste attempts on a lock that's already proven unreachable. **Updated state machine**: The SYNCING → SUSPENDED transition now has a side effect of setting the lock-level flag, and the SUSPENDED → OUT_OF_SYNC transition is driven by the flag being cleared rather than by `_request_sync_check` directly. Actually — should `_request_sync_check` still be able to resume a suspended manager if the flag is cleared? For example: lock is suspended, user changes the PIN. The entity state change fires `_request_sync_check`. At that point the lock-level flag is still set (no coordinator update yet), so the manager stays SUSPENDED. That seems correct — changing the PIN shouldn't retry a dead lock. The lock needs to actually recover first. Does this all track?

It will also convert programming errors (`TypeError`, `AttributeError`, `KeyError`, etc.) into `LockDisconnected`, which tells the sync manager "this is a transient communication issue, please retry" when the real problem might be a bug that will never self-resolve. **Hidden Errors:** `TypeError` (wrong argument types), `AttributeError` (API changes in zwave-js-server-python), `KeyError`, `ValueError` from data processing inside `get_usercode_from_node`, or even `asyncio.CancelledError` (which is a `BaseException` in Python 3.9+ so this one is actually fine, but worth noting). **User Impact:** A programming error in `get_usercode_from_node` would be retried every 5 seconds indefinitely (the `LockDisconnected` path goes to `OUT_OF_SYNC` and retries on next tick), rather than being caught by the generic exception handler that would suspend the lock and create a repair issue. Let me compile the findings. --- ## Error Handling Audit: sync-state-machine branch (second round) ### Finding 1: `_request_sync_check` silently stays IN_SYNC when state resolution fails **Location:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/.claude/worktrees/sync-state-machine/custom_components/lock_code_manager/sync.py`, lines 450-455 **Severity:** MEDIUM **Issue Description:** When `_request_sync_check` is called while in `IN_SYNC` state, and `_resolve_slot_state()` returns `None` (entity not yet available, coordinator data missing, etc.), the condition `slot_state is not None and not self.calculate_in_sync(slot_state)` evaluates to `False`, and the manager stays in `IN_SYNC` without any transition or logging. This means: if a coordinator update or state change fires while entities are temporarily unavailable (e.g., during a reload), the slot stays reported as IN_SYNC even though the actual state is unknown. The next tick will also skip because `IN_SYNC` is in the tick's skip set. The slot will remain stuck in `IN_SYNC` until the next state change event triggers `_request_sync_check` again with entities available. **User Impact:** In practice, this is low-risk because the 5-second tick itself also skips when `IN_SYNC`, so the old `_mark_dirty` design had the same gap -- it would set `_dirty = True` but the display callback (`_update_in_sync_display`) would also silently do nothing when `_in_sync is None` or slot state was unresolvable. The behavioral change here is that the old code would at least set `_dirty = True` to ensure the next tick re-evaluates, whereas now the tick won't run at all. However, the next coordinator update or state change event will re-trigger `_request_sync_check`, so recovery is eventual. This is acceptable given that entity unavailability during normal operation is transient. **Recommendation:** No code change strictly required -- this is a conscious design tradeoff documented in the method docstring ("No-op for LOADING, OUT_OF_SYNC, SYNCING"). However, it would be marginally safer to transition to `OUT_OF_SYNC` when …

Good question — on Python 3.13+ it technically can't, since `CancelledError` is a `BaseException` and `except Exception` won't catch it. The explicit `except asyncio.CancelledError: raise` is purely defensive — it documents the intent ("we know cancellation should propagate") and protects against: 1. Future Python changes (unlikely but possible) 2. Someone adding `except BaseException` during refactoring 3. Making the intent explicit for readers of the code If you'd rather not have dead code, I can remove it. It's a style call — some projects add it as a defensive pattern, others consider it noise.

> AGENT

You're right — `CancelledError` would only fire here if `_perform_sync` (which calls `async_internal_set_usercode` / `async_internal_clear_usercode`) gets cancelled. That happens during HA shutdown or config entry unload, which already tears down the sync manager via `async_stop` (unsubscribes the tick timer). So by the time shutdown cancels tasks, the tick isn't running anymore. The only edge case would be if someone calls `config_entry.async_cancel_task()` on the tick mid-execution, but that's not a pattern used anywhere in this codebase. So it's not a real concern. > DEVELOPER

is there harm in writing syncing to state?

> AGENT

No real harm — `_write_state()` just calls `self._state_writer(self.in_sync)` which triggers `async_write_ha_state()`. Since `in_sync` is `False` for both OUT_OF_SYNC and SYNCING, the binary sensor's `is_on` won't change. The only effect is the `sync_status` attribute flipping from `"out_of_sync"` to `"syncing"` for the duration of the await. The downside is minor: one extra HA state write per sync attempt (and another when transitioning out of SYNCING). For a 5-second tick that's negligible. The upside is the dashboard could show a spinner or different color while actively syncing, which is the whole reason we added the translation. Want me to add it?

Here is my review: --- ## Review: PR #1063 - Zigbee2MQTT Lock Provider **Files reviewed:** - `/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zigbee2mqtt.py` (new, 427 lines) - `/tests/providers/test_zigbee2mqtt.py` (new, 370 lines) - `/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/__init__.py` (modified) - `/custom_components/lock_code_manager/config_flow.py` (modified) - `/custom_components/lock_code_manager/manifest.json` (modified) - `README.md` (modified) --- ### Critical (90-100) **1. `setup_push_subscription` violates the synchronous `@callback` contract -- race condition and broken idempotency** (Confidence: 95) File: `custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zigbee2mqtt.py`, lines ~326-371 The base class defines `setup_push_subscription` as `@callback` (synchronous, must not await). Both Z-Wave JS and Matter implement it fully synchronously -- subscription happens inline and the unsub handle is stored before return. The Zigbee2MQTT implementation does `hass.async_create_task(_async_subscribe())` and returns immediately. This means: 1. `_unsubscribe` is still `None` when the method returns, so the idempotency guard (`if self._unsubscribe is not None: return`) is ineffective. A rapid reconnect cycle (which calls `subscribe_push_updates` again) will spawn duplicate subscriptions. 2. The caller (`subscribe_push_updates` in `_base.py`) expects that on successful return, the subscription IS active. Instead, it might silently fail later inside the task with no error propagation. 3. If the task fails, the `LockDisconnected` exception is swallowed (only logged) and the system believes push is active. **Fix:** HA's `async_subscribe` for MQTT needs to be awaited, but `setup_push_subscription` is `@callback`. The pattern used by other providers is that the subscription API itself is synchronous. Since MQTT's `async_subscribe` is async, you need a different approach. Options: - Override `async_setup()` to do the initial subscription (await `async_subscribe` there and store the unsub). Here is my audit. --- ## Error Handling Audit: PR #1067 (`refactor/base-lock-cleanup`) ### Summary This PR consolidates provider patterns into the base class, tightens error surfaces, and adds logging for previously silent skips. I found **one issue worth flagging** and several areas that are well-handled. The overall direction is good -- it reduces silent failures rather than introducing them. --- ### 1. `unsubscribe_push_updates` catches `ProviderNotImplementedError` silently **Location:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/_base.py`, lines 426-431 **Severity:** MEDIUM (acceptable pattern, but worth verifying intent) **Analysis:** The old code called `self.teardown_push_subscription()` directly, which would raise `ProviderNotImplementedError` if the subclass did not override it. The new code wraps it in a try/except that silently swallows `ProviderNotImplementedError`. This is **justified** because `unsubscribe_push_updates` is called from `async_unload` (line 591) and `_handle_connection_transition` (line 669) -- both guarded by `if self.supports_push`. However, there is a structural question: if `supports_push` is `True` but the provider forgot to override `teardown_push_subscription`, this catch block will hide that programming error at runtime. The symmetric method `subscribe_push_updates` (line 380) explicitly **re-raises** `ProviderNotImplementedError` at line 392-393, which would surface that same class of bug. The asymmetry is intentional if the design allows providers that support push but don't need teardown. But if every push provider must implement teardown, this catch block hides a contract violation. **Recommendation:** Consider whether the `ProviderNotImplementedError` re-raise should mirror `subscribe_push_updates`. If the intent is that teardown is optional for push providers, the current pattern is fine but a comment explaining why would help. If teardown is mandatory for push providers, this catch should re-raise like its subscribe counterpart does. --- ### 2. `async_is_integration_connected` now raises `LockCodeManagerError` -- caller safety verified **Location:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/_base.py`, lines 631-638 **Severity:** NO ISSUE (good change) …

Here's the synthesis: ## E2E Test Strategy for Z2M Provider The key insight from HA core: they have `async_fire_mqtt_message(hass, topic, payload)` which simulates real MQTT messages arriving. Combined with the `mqtt_mock_entry` fixture that sets up a full MQTT integration with a mocked Paho client, we can: 1. **Set up a real MQTT integration** in HA (via `mqtt_mock_entry`) 2. **Fire Z2M discovery payloads** to register the lock device (via `async_fire_mqtt_message` on `homeassistant/device/<ieee>/config`) 3. **Fire Z2M state payloads** to simulate the lock reporting its state (via `async_fire_mqtt_message` on `zigbee2mqtt/<friendly_name>`) 4. **Verify LCM's provider picks up the device**, subscribes to topics, and correctly processes incoming payloads 5. **Verify publishes** by checking `mqtt_client_mock.publish` calls when LCM sets/clears PINs The current Z2M tests mock at the provider level (patching `async_subscribe`, `async_publish`, etc). E2E tests would instead use the HA MQTT test infrastructure so the real MQTT message flow runs end-to-end. ### Payload fixtures needed (from the existing tests and Z2M structure): ```python # Discovery payload (Z2M device-level discovery) Z2M_LOCK_DISCOVERY = { "device": { "identifiers": ["zigbee2mqtt_0x00158d0001234567"], "manufacturer": "Yale", "model": "YMF40A", "name": "Front Door Lock", }, "o": {"name": "Zigbee2MQTT", "sw": "1.50.0"}, "cmps": { "lock": { "platform": "lock", "command_topic": "zigbee2mqtt/Front Door Lock/set/state", "state_topic": "zigbee2mqtt/Front Door Lock", "value_template": "{{ value_json.state }}", "state_locked": "LOCKED", "state_unlocked": "UNLOCKED", "unique_id": "0x00158d0001234567_lock", } }, } # State payload with users/PINs Z2M_LOCK_STATE = { "state": "LOCKED", "users": { "1": {"status": "enabled", "pin_code": "1234"}, "2": {"status": "enabled", "pin_code": "5678"}, "3": {"status": "disabled"}, }, } # pin_code GET response Z2M_PIN_CODE_RESPONSE = { "pin_code": {"user": 1, "user_enabled": True, "pin_code": "1234"} } ``` ### Key dependencies: - `pytest-homeassistant-custom-component` already provides `mqtt_mock_entry` and `async_fire_mqtt_message` (inherited from HA core test utils) This is a significant test infrastructure piece — want me to create a branch and start implementing it, or would you like to discuss …

Honestly, it's a good sign — but also a bit suspicious. A few reasons we haven't found bugs: 1. **The code was already heavily tested before we started.** The existing unit tests (600+) covered the individual components well. The recent sync state machine refactor (#1062) was designed carefully with multiple review rounds. The fact that the adversarial tests pass confirms the design is sound, not that the tests are weak. 2. **MockLCMLock is too cooperative.** It never fails, never delays, never returns unexpected data. Real locks do all of those things. The adversarial tests stress component *boundaries* but with a perfectly behaved lock. The Z2M issue (#1061) that started this session was a real-world failure mode (Z-Wave node presumed dead) — that class of bug lives in the provider-to-real-hardware boundary, which we can't test without mocking specific failure modes. 3. **We're testing the state machine, not the timing.** The tick-based architecture means tests fire ticks manually (`async_fire_time_changed`). Real bugs often involve timing — two ticks racing, a push update arriving mid-tick, a coordinator refresh overlapping with a sync operation. Our tests are sequential by nature. 4. **The refactoring session that preceded this was thorough.** You caught the circuit breaker reset bug, the Z-Wave error classification, the `_disable_slot` blast radius — all during design review, before code was written. The bugs were fixed before they were committed. The real bugs are probably in: - **Specific lock firmware behavior** (the Z2M `expose_pin` edge cases, Matter's Aqara quirks) - **Timing under load** (30 locks, 10 slots each, all polling simultaneously) - **HA lifecycle edge cases** (config entry migration, HA restart mid-sync, integration reload races) Want me to write timing/race-condition focused tests, or move on to something else?

> AGENT

Let me think about what race conditions are actually possible in the single-threaded HA event loop. The key insight: HA is single-threaded, so "races" happen at `await` points — when one coroutine yields, another can run. Let me identify the specific await points in the sync/coordinator code that could allow interleaving:

> AGENT

The critical await points during a tick are: - `await self._perform_sync(slot_state)` — calls the lock provider - `await self._coordinator.async_refresh()` — polls the coordinator after sync - `await self._disable_slot(...)` — disables the slot During any of these awaits, coordinator listeners can fire, entity state changes can process, and other ticks could theoretically run. Let me design tests that exploit these windows:

The keys have been `type` and `index` since the very first commit that introduced this service (ee05f145307, Feb 28). They were **never** `credential_type`/`credential_index`. So the bug has existed since the Matter provider was written. If your live testing worked, it means `async_get_usercodes` returned all slots as EMPTY (no occupied slots detected), but the sync manager still worked because: 1. **Push events populated the coordinator.** The `LockUserChange` handler (`_handle_lock_user_change`) correctly pushes `UNREADABLE_CODE`/`EMPTY` to the coordinator when credentials are added/cleared. This path doesn't use the broken credential key parsing. 2. **The initial poll returned empty, but push events immediately corrected it.** On startup, `async_get_usercodes` found zero occupied slots (due to the bug), but the push subscription was already active, and any subsequent changes via the sync manager triggered `LockUserChange` events that updated the coordinator correctly. 3. **`_last_set_pin` handles write-only locks.** For Matter (write-only PINs), after LCM sets a code, `_last_set_pin` tracks what was set. The sync manager uses this to determine in-sync status when the code is `UNREADABLE_CODE`. So even with a broken initial poll, the system converges to in-sync after the first set operation. The bug is real — `async_get_usercodes` returns wrong data — but the system self-heals through push events. It would only be noticeable if you looked at the coordinator data immediately after startup before any sync operations ran, or if you had codes on the lock from a previous session that LCM didn't set (external codes would never appear as occupied). Want me to revert the key change and keep the original keys, or keep the fix since it's correct even though the system compensated for the bug?

The sync loop is real — sets happening every ~30 seconds, cycling through all slots on both locks. Let me trace the full cycle to understand why `_last_set_pin` isn't preventing re-sets:

> AGENT

**Found the real bug.** Two problems: 1. **`ReadTimeout` from Schlage API causes suspension** — `ReadTimeout` is not a `LockDisconnected` or `LockOperationFailed`, so it falls through to the generic `except Exception` handler which suspends the lock. But a timeout is a transient error that should be retried, not suspended. 2. **`schlage.add_code` fails with "already exists" → `LockOperationFailed` → infinite retry** — The Schlage provider tries to `add_code` but the code already exists (from a previous successful set). The Schlage API rejects it with "A PIN code with the name [LCM:1] already exists." This is caught as `LockOperationFailed` which maps to retry on next tick — creating an infinite loop. The root cause: after a successful set, the Schlage provider should be using `_async_tag_unmanaged_codes` or the update flow (delete + re-add) rather than `add_code`. The code already exists from the prior set, but the sync manager thinks it needs to set it again because `_last_set_pin` was lost (either from restart or from the suspension clearing state). Let me look at the Schlage `async_set_usercode` to understand the flow:

> AGENT

Now I see the issue. At line 318: when the code already exists with the same tagged name, the provider does delete → add. But at line 336: when there's **no existing code found** (because `_async_get_codes` returned fresh data where the code was already deleted by a previous failed attempt), it just calls `_async_add_code` — which fails with "already exists" because Schlage's API is eventually consistent and the delete hasn't propagated yet. Actually, the deeper issue is at line 301: `_async_get_codes` makes a fresh API call to Schlage. If the previous set succeeded but the coordinator refresh failed (or timed out), the code IS on the lock, but `_last_set_pin` was never set (because the set operation raised `LockOperationFailed` from the "already exists" error from `_async_add_code`). Wait — let me re-read the logs. The error is `"A PIN code with the name "[LCM:1] X222222X" already exists on the lock"`. This means the code IS already there from a previous set, but the Schlage provider is trying to add it again with `_async_add_code` instead of the delete-then-add path. This means line 301's `_async_get_codes` didn't find the existing code. The issue is that `_async_get_codes` calls `schlage.get_codes` which queries the Schlage cloud. The cloud might return stale data (code not yet visible), so the provider thinks there's no existing code and tries `add_code` — which fails because the cloud IS aware of the code for writes but not for reads (eventual consistency). The fix should be: when `_async_add_code` fails with "already exists", the provider should fall back to the delete-then-add path rather than propagating the error.

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation. Summary: 1. Primary Request and Intent: The user (raman325) is the maintainer of Lock Code Manager, a Home Assistant custom integration for managing lock PINs across multiple lock types. The most recent work is fixing the Schlage sync loop caused by cloud API eventual consistency and uncaught ReadTimeout errors. 2. Key Technical Concepts: - SyncState enum state machine (LOADING, IN_SYNC, OUT_OF_SYNC, SYNCING, SUSPENDED) replacing boolean flags - Per-lock suspension via coordinator.suspend_slot_sync_mgrs() - sync_status attribute on binary sensor (in_sync, out_of_sync, syncing, suspended) - slot_suspended repair issue per-lock - Matter lock_helpers direct API calls replacing HA service calls - SetCredentialFailedError typed duplicate detection vs string matching - CodeRejectedError vs LockDisconnected distinction (lock rejected ≠ disconnected) - MqttMessageBus for Z2M E2E testing - Full LCM async_setup_entry lifecycle testing pattern - Schlage cloud API eventual consistency (get_codes vs add_code divergence) - ReadTimeout (OSError) not caught by HomeAssistantError handler - Python 3.14 PEP 758 (except without parentheses) - ruff target-version = "py314" for correct formatting - _ensure_entities_ready allowing STATE_UNKNOWN for optional/inactive entities - pytest-homeassistant-custom-component version coupling with HA 3. Files and Code Sections: - `custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/schlage.py` - Currently being modified for the Schlage sync loop fix - async_set_usercode was just simplified from try/except "already exists" fallback to always-delete-then-add: ```python # Clear any existing code on this slot before adding. The PR is now updated with the simplified `async_set_usercode` pattern. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Set deduplication**: Using `{n for n in (existing_full_name, tagged_name) if n}` elegantly handles three cases: (1) no existing code → only `tagged_name` deleted, (2) name unchanged → same string deduplicated to 1 delete, (3) name changed → both old and new names deleted. The set comprehension also filters `None` values. **Eventual consistency defense**: The "always delete first" pattern is a common cloud API resilience technique. Since the Schlage API's `get_codes` may lag behind `add_code`, we can't rely on the read to tell us what exists — so we speculatively delete and swallow "not found" errors. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

OK, I've analyzed the 2.3.3 log and found the root cause. The fix we shipped (delete-then-add) is **necessary but not sufficient**. But for Schlage, "already exists" with our tagged name means the code IS on the lock — it should be treated as success. Let me implement both fixes.

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Root cause**: The sync loop persists because of a timing mismatch between the write path (Schlage cloud) and read path (get_codes). After a successful `add_code`, `get_codes` may still return stale data showing the slot as EMPTY. The current `calculate_in_sync` only trusts `_last_set_pin` for `UNREADABLE_CODE`, not for `EMPTY`. When the stale read returns EMPTY, line 332 immediately returns `False`, ignoring that we just successfully set the code. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` **Fix 1**: Trust `_last_set_pin` when the lock reports EMPTY but we recently set a PIN.

PR #1090 is ready: https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/pull/1090 `★ Insight ──────────────────���──────────────────` **Eventual consistency manifests in TWO directions**: The Schlage cloud API is eventually consistent on both the write path (`add_code` sees old entries after a `delete_code`) AND the read path (`get_codes` returns stale data after an `add_code`). The original fix only addressed write-side consistency. The user's 2.3.3 log revealed the read-side problem: after a successful set, the verification refresh sees EMPTY → sync decides it's out of sync → re-sets → "already exists" → loop. **The `_last_set_pin` pattern is the key**: It serves as the sync manager's "write-side memory" — when the read can't be trusted (UNREADABLE_CODE, stale EMPTY), the sync manager falls back to "did I successfully set this PIN?" rather than relying solely on what the lock reports. Extending this trust to EMPTY (not just UNREADABLE_CODE) closes the consistency gap. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

PR #1091 created: https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/pull/1091 `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Rebuild vs. catch-up tradeoff**: With 116 commits behind and 3 API-breaking changes (`get_entry_data` → `managed_slots` property, `async_set_usercode` signature, test directory restructuring), rebuilding was the right call. The merge had 3 conflicts in core files, and the old PR's package structure (4 files in `providers/zha/`) was inconsistent with every other provider being a single module. The rebuild took ~15 minutes vs. what would have been 30+ minutes of conflict resolution and interface patching. **Provider registration is lightweight**: Adding a new provider to LCM only requires 3 touchpoints: (1) the provider module, (2) one line in `INTEGRATIONS_CLASS_MAP`, (3) one line in `manifest.json` after_dependencies. The `BaseLock` dataclass pattern means the constructor signature is inherited — no boilerplate. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Here's the test plan from the Matter lock work, adapted for your Zigbee testing: ## Completed Phases (Matter - Aqara U300) 1. **Baseline** — empty lock state validation 2. **Direct set/get round-trip** — basic read/write 3. **Duplicate code detection** — hardware rejects duplicate PINs 4. **LCM config entry setup + restart survival** — persistence 5. *(skipped/combined)* 6. **Disable/enable cycle** — toggle lock access 7. **Duplicate PIN via LCM** — DuplicateCodeError path 8. **Config entry deletion** — codes persist after removal (by design) ## Still Remaining (untested on any lock) 1. **PIN change while in sync** — change PIN via text entity, verify re-sync clears old + sets new 2. **Multiple config entries sharing same lock** — "All Locks" + specific entry conflict 3. **Hard refresh drift detection** — shorten `hard_refresh_interval`, verify it catches keypad-set code 4. **Config flow re-add** — delete and re-add, verify picks up existing codes ## Zigbee-Specific Considerations - **Z2M first, then ZHA** — same physical lock, two different provider paths - Z2M provider uses MQTT messages; ZHA uses zigpy DoorLock cluster commands directly - Zigbee locks can read PINs back (unlike Matter's UNREADABLE_CODE / Schlage's write-only), so sync verification should be more straightforward - Programming event support varies by lock model — test whether the lock fires events on keypad code changes or needs drift detection fallback - Code slot events (lock/unlock with user ID) should work on both Z2M and ZHA if the lock supports operation event notifications What Zigbee lock model did you get?

> DEVELOPER

kwikset 914

> AGENT

Good choice — the Kwikset 914 is one of the most common Zigbee locks and has solid ZCL DoorLock cluster support. It should support both PIN read/write and operation event notifications (keypad lock/unlock with user ID). A few things worth noting for testing: - **PIN readback** — the 914 returns actual PINs (not masked), so `async_get_usercodes` should return real codes, not `UNREADABLE_CODE`. This gives us full sync verification unlike Matter/Schlage. - **Programming events** — the 914 may or may not support programming event notifications (PIN add/delete from keypad). This is what the `_async_check_programming_event_support` detection in the ZHA provider tests. If it doesn't, the hourly drift detection kicks in. - **Slot count** — the 914 typically supports up to 30 user code slots (slots 1-30), with slot 0 reserved for the master code. - **Z2M vs ZHA** — when testing Z2M first, you'll pair it to your Z2M coordinator. You'll need to unpair and re-pair to ZHA for the second round of testing (can't be on both simultaneously since Zigbee devices only join one network). When you're ready to start testing tomorrow, the plan would be: 1. Pair to Z2M, run phases 1-8 against the Z2M provider 2. Unpair from Z2M, pair to ZHA, run the same phases against the ZHA provider 3. Then tackle the 4 remaining items that haven't been tested on any lock Want me to save a memory note about the Kwikset 914 and the testing plan?

For the Kwikset 914 ZHA pairing: 1. **In HA**: Settings → Devices & Services → ZHA → Configure → Add Device (this opens the permit join window, usually 60 seconds) 2. **On the lock**: Press the "Program" button on the interior panel (small button, usually near the battery compartment). Some models require pressing it once to enter programming mode, then pressing a specific sequence. For the 914, it's typically just pressing the "A" button on the interior once. 3. The lock should appear in ZHA within 30 seconds If it was previously paired to another Zigbee network (Z2M or another coordinator), you may need to factory reset the Zigbee radio on the lock first — usually hold the "A" button for 10+ seconds until it beeps/flashes. For testing strategy — I'd suggest **prod instance** for now since: - The user on #1083 just confirmed our Schlage fix works on prod, and that workflow (debug logs → analyze) is proven - Setting up a local HA instance with ZHA over network adds complexity we don't need yet - We mainly need to verify the ZHA provider discovers the lock, reads codes, and sets/clears correctly - If something breaks, it's just a test lock with test codes — no risk Once paired, you can verify it shows up with `ha` MCP tools or SSH. Coordinator refreshes → reads `9999` back from lock → confirms match → `IN_SYNC` (green) The `SYNCING` state is brief but visible because the ZHA provider has to make a round-trip to the lock (set_pin_code → get_pin_code to verify). This is working as designed — the state machine is `OUT_OF_SYNC → SYNCING → IN_SYNC`. What do you want to test next?

> DEVELOPER

based on our data, what's the right tick size? Five seconds feels too long but I don't want to risk not processing everything within the tick. Is that even a risk?

> AGENT

Currently 5 seconds. The tick is not the bottleneck — here's why it's safe to reduce: The tick itself is very lightweight when nothing needs to happen. It's just: 1. Check `_state` — if `IN_SYNC`, `SYNCING`, or `SUSPENDED`, return immediately (line 512-513) 2. Only does real work when `LOADING` or `OUT_OF_SYNC` The actual lock operations (set/clear/get) are rate-limited separately by `_min_operation_delay` (2 seconds between operations). So even with a 1-second tick, the lock won't be hammered — the rate limiter serializes operations. The risk of a tick being "too fast" is minimal because: - The tick is per-slot, but they all share the same operation lock + rate limiter - If a sync operation is in progress (`SYNCING`), the tick no-ops - State change listeners already trigger `_request_sync_check` immediately — the tick is just a safety net For responsiveness, **2 seconds** would feel snappy without any real downside. The only cost is slightly more frequent no-op checks, which are negligible (a few attribute lookups per tick per slot). Want to drop it to 2 or 3 seconds?

Matter pushes `UNREADABLE_CODE` because it can't read PINs back. So the optimistic value varies by provider. We can still centralize this — the base can call `push_update` with whatever the provider returned, but the "what to push" differs. Actually, looking at this more carefully: the providers push different values: - **Z-Wave JS**: `{code_slot: usercode}` (actual PIN) - **ZHA**: `{code_slot: usercode}` (actual PIN) - **Z2M**: `{code_slot: str(usercode)}` (actual PIN) - **Matter**: `{code_slot: SlotCode.UNREADABLE_CODE}` (can't read PINs) For set, 3 out of 4 push the actual usercode, but Matter can't. For clear, all push `SlotCode.EMPTY`. The base could handle the common case: push `usercode` for set, `EMPTY` for clear. Matter would need to override to push `UNREADABLE_CODE` instead. But that's actually more complex than the current pattern — Matter would need to either suppress the base's push and do its own, or the base would need a hook like `_get_optimistic_set_value()`. Not worth the abstraction for 4 providers. The current pattern is clear and each provider explicitly documents what it pushes. I'd leave it as-is. The pattern is consistent and explicit across all providers. Moving it to the base would require either: 1. A `_get_optimistic_set_value(code_slot, usercode)` hook that Matter overrides to return `UNREADABLE_CODE` — adds indirection for no real benefit 2. The base always pushing `usercode` and Matter suppressing + re-pushing — worse Four identical lines across four providers is clearer than a premature abstraction. The comment in the base (`Push-based providers handle this via push_update()`) already documents the contract. I'd leave it.

The ZHA spec status codes include things like "duplicate code", "memory full", or "code does not meet policy". Wrapping all of these as `LockDisconnected` causes the sync engine to retry indefinitely instead of disabling the slot and raising `CodeRejectedError` / `DuplicateCodeError`. `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zha.py`, lines 272–273 and 293–294: ```python if hasattr(result, "status") and result.status != 0: raise LockDisconnected(f"set_pin_code failed: status {result.status}") ``` The ZCL DoorLock `SetPinCode` response status `0` = Success; any other value means the lock refused …

This means if zigpy changes its response format, or the lock returns an error response object that has a different shape, existing PIN codes will appear to be empty. The sync engine will then either try to re-program the code (wasted churn) or, worse, if the slot is supposed to be cleared, it will think it already is. The raw result IS logged at debug level (line 235-239), but there is no indication that parsing fell through to the default branch. Someone debugging "why did the sync engine think slot 3 was empty when it has a code?" would need to know to look at the raw debug output and manually reason about the parse logic. **User Impact:** A code that exists on the lock could be reported as empty, causing unnecessary sync operations or masking the fact that the lock's firmware responded in an unexpected way. **Recommendation:** Log a warning when the response falls through to the default return, so there is a clear signal that something unexpected happened: ```python @staticmethod def _parse_pin_response(result: Any) -&gt; tuple[int, str]: """Extract (user_status, pin_code) from a get_pin_code response.""" if hasattr(result, "user_status"): pin = getattr(result, "code", "") or "" if isinstance(pin, bytes): pin = pin.decode("utf-8", errors="ignore") return result.user_status, str(pin) if isinstance(result, …

Right — neither ISY994 nor SimpliSafe has a `get_codes`/`get_usercode` service. They're write-only: - **ISY994**: `set_zwave_lock_user_code`, `delete_zwave_lock_user_code` — no read - **SimpliSafe**: `set_pin`, `remove_pin` — no read Without a way to read codes back, a provider would have the same problem as Schlage (write-only PINs → `UNREADABLE_CODE` → can't verify sync). Not viable as providers unless we find another way to read codes (e.g., directly through the underlying library like we did with zigpy for ZHA).

You're right. The PIN's identity in LCM is `config_entry_id + slot_num`, not `lock_entity_id`. > AGENT

Now update the caller in `BaseLock`:

> AGENT

Hmm, you're right — `config_entry_id` changes if the user deletes and recreates the config entry. And it's an opaque ID that doesn't help correlate. But using all lock entity IDs sorted would mean the hash is stable across config entry recreation as long as the same locks are managed. But wait — that introduces a problem: if you add/remove a lock from the config entry, all the hashes change. And the `mask_pin` caller in `BaseLock` is per-lock (it doesn't know about other locks in the config entry). Let me think about what the actual need is: 1. **In diagnostics**: support needs to see "slot 1 configured PIN == slot 1 coordinator code on lock A == slot 1 coordinator code on lock B". So the salt should produce the same hash for the same PIN+slot regardless of which lock. 2. **In debug logs**: developer needs to correlate PINs within a log stream for a single lock operation. Actually, the simplest correct salt is just `instance_id + slot_num + pin`. No lock or config entry in the salt at all. The PIN on slot 1 is "1234" — that's deterministic. The only reason for extra salt is to prevent cross-instance correlation (instance_id) and cross-slot correlation (slot_num). We don't need lock or config entry in the salt because: - Same PIN on slot 1 across locks SHOULD hash the same (it's the same configured PIN) - Same PIN on slot 1 vs slot 2 SHOULD hash differently (different slots, might be a coincidence)

Done. `mask_pin` now salts by `instance_id + slot_num` only. Same PIN on slot 1 produces the same hash whether it's on lock A, lock B, or in the configured slot config — so diagnostics can directly compare "is the configured PIN the same as what the coordinator sees on each lock?"

> DEVELOPER

so same PIN on two slot 1s for different subsets of locks would have the same mask. I think that's acceptable right?

> AGENT

Yes, that's acceptable. The scenario is: two config entries, each managing different locks, both with slot 1 set to "1234". They'd produce the same masked value. In practice this is a feature, not a bug — if someone has two config entries managing different locks with the same PIN on the same slot number, seeing the same masked value in both diagnostics confirms "yes, these are the same PIN." That's useful information, not a leak. The only way this could be a concern is if an attacker had diagnostics from two different config entries and wanted to correlate PINs across them — but they'd need access to both diagnostics files, and the instance_id salt already prevents cross-instance correlation. Within the same instance, knowing "slot 1 on config A has the same PIN as slot 1 on config B" isn't sensitive — the user set them both.

> DEVELOPER

add corepack enable. what is the pre-commit legacy?

> AGENT

The `pre-commit.legacy` is from Codespaces — it sets up its own git hook at `.git/hooks/pre-commit`. When `pre-commit install` runs, it finds that existing hook and renames it to `pre-commit.legacy` instead of overwriting it, then runs both. That's why we see "Running in migration mode with existing hooks at .git/hooks/pre-commit.legacy". Since we already `git config --unset-all core.hooksPath`, we should also remove the Codespaces hook before installing ours.

> AGENT

Now fix the legacy hook issue in the setup script:

> AGENT

Pushed. To your question — yes, it's a Codespaces/GitHub thing. Codespaces installs its own pre-commit hook at `.git/hooks/pre-commit` to support features like auto-GPG signing and their own git workflow tooling. When pre-commit (or prek) runs `install`, it finds that existing hook and goes into "migration mode" — renaming it to `.legacy` and running both. Our script now removes both before installing a clean hook.

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

> AGENT

No. The Codespaces hook does two things: GPG commit signing setup and some telemetry. Neither is needed for our workflow — we don't use GPG signing, and Codespaces telemetry works fine without the hook. The hook is a convenience, not a requirement. If Codespaces ever needs something from its hook, it'll still work through `core.hooksPath` (which Codespaces re-sets on container start). But we unset that too — and that's also fine because Codespaces' git integration works at a higher level than git hooks.

> AGENT

Just a more detailed PIN validation TODO — expanded the bullet point with implementation details (opt-in toggle, blocklist/allowlist regex rules, shared `validate_pin()` function). > DEVELOPER

could this be accomplished as a blueprint instead? Maybe one that takes in a config entry + slot + list of regex's, and an automation that checks when the state of the PIN value changes to something that's the list of regex's and it disables the slot and notifies the user or something to that effect. I guess that's not as nice as blocking immediately on the service call

> AGENT

Right — the blueprint approach would work but it's reactive: the PIN gets set on the lock, *then* the blueprint fires, disables the slot, and clears the code. There's a window where an invalid PIN is active on the lock. Blocking on the service call (config flow / options flow / websocket) means the invalid PIN never reaches the lock at all. That's meaningfully better for security — a simple PIN like "1234" would be rejected before it's ever programmed. But there's a middle ground: the duplicate PIN detection we already have works at the sync layer (`_check_duplicate_code` in `_pre_execute_checks`). PIN validation could follow the same pattern — check the PIN right before sending it to the lock, and raise `CodeRejectedError` if it fails validation. No config flow changes needed, no websocket changes, and the PIN never makes it to the lock. The blueprint approach is still useful as an *additional* layer for custom rules that don't belong in the core integration (company-specific PIN policies, etc.). But basic validation (simple PINs, format checks) belongs in the sync pre-execute path. I'd leave the TODO as-is for now — it's a nice-to-have, not urgent. Want me to update it with this layered approach note, or move on?

> AGENT

Yes — HA config flows support a `progress` step. You show a progress page while a background task runs, and the flow advances when it's done. But it's binary (in progress / done), not a granular progress bar with counts. For granular "3/6 slots cleared" feedback, the options are: 1. **Progress step with description placeholders** — the progress page can show a message, and you can update the description with current counts via `async_update_reload_and_abort` or by storing state. But the page doesn't auto-refresh — the user has to wait for the task to complete. 2. **Persistent notification** — fire a notification as each slot clears, then dismiss when done. Works but noisy. 3. **Repair issue** — create a temporary repair that shows progress. Overkill. Realistically, the progress step with a "Clearing codes on X locks..." message is the right UX. The user sees something is happening and waits. If individual slot clears take a long time (like Schlage's 30s per API call), the total could be minutes — so showing *something* is important even if it's not granular. Want me to file an issue for this, or tackle it now?

> DEVELOPER

what does this do? num_locks = sum( 1 for codes in self._all_codes.values() if any( codes.get(s, SlotCode.EMPTY) != SlotCode.EMPTY for s in self._slots_to_clear ) )

> AGENT

Counts how many locks have at least one non-empty code on the slots being cleared. It's for the progress message: "Clearing existing codes from 3 slot(s) across **2 lock(s)**." `self._all_codes` is `{lock_entity_id: {slot_num: code}}`. The comprehension iterates each lock's codes and checks if any of the slots-to-clear have a non-empty code on that lock. A lock with no codes on those slots wouldn't need clearing, so it's excluded from the count. Example: 2 locks, clearing slots 1 and 2. - Lock A: slot 1 has "1234", slot 2 is empty → counted (1 non-empty slot) - Lock B: slot 1 is empty, slot 2 is empty → not counted Result: "1 lock" in the message.

> DEVELOPER

we are really just supposed to loop on the progress return until the task is done?

> AGENT

Yes — that's how HA's progress step works. The frontend polls the flow endpoint repeatedly. Each poll calls `async_step_existing_codes_clear` again: 1. First call: task is None → create task, return `show_progress` (spinner shown to user) 2. Frontend polls again → task exists but not done → return `show_progress` again (spinner continues) 3. Frontend polls again → task is done → return `show_progress_done` → flow advances The frontend handles the polling loop automatically — you don't write the loop, HA's flow engine does. The step function just checks `self._clear_task.done()` each time it's called. Looking at our code again though, there might be an issue: when the task completes between creating it and checking `.done()`, HA sees `show_progress_done` on the same call that created the task and skips the spinner entirely. That's what happened in our tests (mock is instant). For real locks with 30s API calls, the user will see the spinner.

> AGENT

True. The current pattern treats clearing as one opaque task. But we know the individual operations: N slots × M locks. We could update the progress message as each one completes. HA's `async_show_progress` supports `description_placeholders` — if we update those between polls, the message changes. The flow step gets re-called on each poll, so we can read a counter that the task updates.

> DEVELOPER

should we note that operations can take up to 30 seconds? why is it that long?

> AGENT

The 30 seconds is Schlage-specific — their cloud API (`api.allegion.yonomi.cloud`) is slow. Each `add_code`/`delete_code` call goes: HA → Schlage cloud → Schlage WiFi lock → back. The logs from issue #1083 showed individual operations taking 20-30 seconds consistently. Other providers are much faster: - Z-Wave JS: ~1-2 seconds (local, direct radio) - ZHA: ~1-2 seconds (local, zigpy cluster command) - Matter: ~3-5 seconds (local Thread/WiFi) - Z2M: ~2-3 seconds (local MQTT) So the note should be general — "some locks may take longer depending on the integration."

> DEVELOPER

should self._clear_total = len(slots to clear) * len(lock instances)?

> AGENT

Not exactly — `len(slots) * len(locks)` would count slot/lock pairs where the slot is already empty on that lock. We skip those (the `if codes.get(slot_num, SlotCode.EMPTY) == SlotCode.EMPTY: continue` in `_clear_existing_slot`). So the counter would show "6/6" but only 4 actually ran, making it jump from 4/6 to done. But honestly, that's overthinking it. The current comprehension counts the exact number of operations that will happen, so the counter is accurate. The difference only matters if some locks have codes on different slots — which is unlikely since LCM manages the same slots across all locks. If you'd prefer the simpler `len * len` for readability and accept the slight overcount, it works fine — the counter just finishes faster than expected. Up to you.

> DEVELOPER

wait so is no paranthesis an issue?

> AGENT

No — `except ValueError, TypeError:` without parentheses is **valid Python 3.14** syntax thanks to PEP 758. It's not the Python 2 meaning (which was `except ValueError as TypeError:`). PEP 758 explicitly allows the comma-separated form as syntactic sugar for the tuple. All our tests pass with it, the module imports fine, and ruff formats it this way intentionally when `target-version = "py314"`. The only "issue" is that tools and reviewers that don't know about PEP 758 flag it as a Python 2 error. That's a false positive — Copilot, the code reviewer agents we ran, and anyone reading the code who learned Python before 3.14 will think it's wrong. But it's not.
